# RUNNER-SPEC v2.18: entries, calls, sessions, subscriptions and operation of a place (lane runner-spec-1020)

**Tree:** `62f15988b85ca4afdb6d37e0ff324d9072274615` (detached), 18 crates. The checker was rebuilt there (BUILD SUCCEEDED). Every v2.2 probe was checked and run on it. **v2.3** adds R0's built figures (runner-build-1033, landed bf7cf64f, measured there and at 71b306f0) and three probes run at 62f15988 (p19 split and few-registration variants).

**Store:** written against **STORE-SPEC v2.7** (/workspace/code/hirsel-briefs/store-spec-1001/STORE-SPEC.md, CHANGES-v2.4 to v2.7: G1–G4, H1–H5, I1–I5, J1). The landed store is S1 (603a2274); S1.1 (`look`, `changes`, Shape reads) is being built.

**Review answered:** runner-spec-review-1028/ADVICE.md (findings 1–7 and every "Model and law gaps" line).

Evidence marks:
- **RUN**: run or checked here;
- **READ**: taken from a cited file;
- **ESTIMATE**: computed from RUN or READ parts;
- **GUESS**: an estimate with no measured part.

**v2.11** writes the R2 contract that WIRE-SPEC v2 (wire-spec-1059, after wire-attack-1060) needs from this spec. It covers the publisher-to-owner event and its accounting, normalized rows (F10), `complete` (F11), `DONE_MAX` and the outcome reserve (F12), Cover-only retention (F4), and gone-key slots (F9). It **corrects** 1059's reserve: the slot table alone does not bound the outcome rows owed to a session. It adds five laws. R2-g, R2-h and R2-i (admission, launch identity, resume) are not in v2.11. The models were not re-run; the new owner bound is **UNTESTED** until its R1 case. The v2.10 text is RUNNER-SPEC-v2.10.md.

**v2.8** applies Sam's gavel 11 (duplex owned halves, option A; net-proposal-999 §3 as amended by net-proposal-amend-1053): a session is one owned task group per connection. Text only, no model re-run: the models' session is one sequential actor, and the owner task is that actor. The v2.7 text is RUNNER-SPEC-v2.7.md.

**v2.6** rules on L3's oracle (runner-build-1033, followups.md 09:50). v2.5's oracle, a harness `Sender` carried in H, cannot be built: `handles` makes H from the Db alone (rule 12). The law now counts the body's `TRY_SEND` events through the CONC-10 event sink, on every backend. Text only; no model behaviour changed, so no model was re-run. The v2.5 text is RUNNER-SPEC-v2.5.md.

**v2.5** answers review 1042 (runner-build-review-1042/ATTACK.md): one send received two Outcomes in the built runner, and no check observed it. Rule 5 now defines a send and its one Outcome. The calls and subscription models gain exactly-once checks (`models/calls25_model.py`, `models/subs25_model.py`), and every model was re-run. The v2.4 text is RUNNER-SPEC-v2.4.md.

The v2.2 and v2.3 texts are RUNNER-SPEC-v2.2.md and RUNNER-SPEC-v2.3.md. **v2.4** rules on R1's five gaps (runner-build-1033, followups.md 2026-11-16 "R1 gaps") against STORE-SPEC v2.9 and REPLAY-SPEC v2.1, by reading; no model behaviour changed, so no model was re-run. Models are in `models/`, their outputs in `models/evidence/`. Probes are in `programs/`, their outcomes in `probes.txt`. The v2 and v2.1 change tables are in CHANGES-HISTORY.md, and the v2.1 text is RUNNER-SPEC-v2.1.md.

## CHANGES-v2.18 (astra's third attack, ATTACK-3: 7 BLOCKER, 8 MAJOR, 1 MINOR; the orchestrator's pass-4 method)

Three passes of patching took the blockers from 4 to 6 to 7, so this pass starts again from two questions, each answered in its own design. Neither design blocks the other.

- **Release order** answers B1, B2, B3 and M1–M4. It starts from prior art.
- **The `serve` store contracts** answer B4–B7, M5 and M6, and also take M7, M8 and m1. They are STORE-SPEC v2.29 AM1–AM8.

The release design needs exactly three store hooks, listed under "Interface between the two designs" below. v2.17's `Latest[Authority]` roots, `Scope`, `Authority`, epochs, prior digests, the session watch, G2 step 0's fences and the owner's `Stale(e, s)` rule are deleted.

### Release order: prior art (each file opened; shallow clones in /tmp/ref-*)

| System | How a permission change reaches live subscriptions | How it reaches a reply already computed |
|---|---|---|
| Convex (`get-convex/convex-backend`) | **Identity changes.** Each session's sync worker drops every subscription and re-runs the whole query set at one new timestamp (`crates/sync/src/worker.rs:948-964`, version `{ts, identity}` at `:1006-1012`). **Data changes.** A change that touches what a permission check read re-runs the query through its read set, like any other dependency. | A retried mutation returns its stored result with no authorization recheck (`crates/application/src/application_function_runner/mod.rs:2136-2163`, `check_mutation_status`). |
| Hasura (`hasura/graphql-engine`) | A live query is re-polled per cohort, and the cohort's session variables are fixed in its key (`server/src-lib/Hasura/GraphQL/Execute/Subscription/Plan.hs:184-186`). Permissions are evaluated in SQL on each poll. JWT expiry closes the connection (`Transport/WebSocket/Server.hs:788, 821-823`). | — (not read) |
| Supabase Realtime (`supabase/realtime`) | **Postgres changes.** RLS is evaluated per WAL record for each subscriber role (`lib/realtime/tenants/repo/migrations/20260709120000_fix_apply_rls_filter_role_leak.ex:163-195`). **Token refresh.** The channel's policies are re-evaluated, and a revoked read shuts the channel down (`lib/realtime_web/channels/realtime_channel.ex:638-678, 1210-1215`). | — (not read) |
| Firestore (`firebase/firebase-js-sdk`, client only) | The server removes a target with a cause, and the client drops that listener with the error (`packages/firestore/src/remote/remote_store.ts:612-620`; `core/sync_engine_impl.ts:1593-1599`). The server side was not read. | — (not read) |
| Zero (`rocicorp/mono`) | **Auth update.** The view syncer re-validates the connection, then re-transforms every query (`packages/zero-cache/src/services/view-syncer/view-syncer.ts:1650-1664`). **Failed validation.** The connection is invalidated (`:4450-4455`). Zero's data-permission path and Replicache were not read. | — (not read) |

**What they share:** each re-evaluates per subscription or per connection, and resets or closes on an identity change. None orders one data revocation across in-flight units of several sessions, which ruling (ii) asks for (DECISIONS 2026-11-18). Convex is the closest analogue, with its re-run at one timestamp. Its replies are not rechecked.

**Not read:** Replicache, Zero's data-permission rules, and Firestore's server (only its JS client is open source). The comparison above rests on what was read.

### Release order: the two simplest sound shapes, against v2.17

- **(b) Reset every affected session.** Alone, this is not sound across writers. "Affected" is decided per session, so the resets reach writers one by one. That is ATTACK-3 B1's second paragraph: A's writer sees its reset while B's writer still accepts old content. (b) is still the right answer for *content* once a unit is known stale, and it is what Convex and Zero do. The design below uses it there.
- **(a) One global authority counter, checked at handoff.** This gives every writer one effective point: the instant the counter is stored. It is the shape adopted, with two changes.
  1. **No acknowledgement wait.** A writer loads the counter *after* it takes a unit and *before* the unit's first byte. A unit that loaded the old value was accepted before the effective point and may finish, which ruling (ii) allows. A unit that loads the new value is refused. So nothing has to wait for writers to pass the new value.
  2. **The counter moves before the commit is observable, not at durability.** The store publishes it inside the commit that writes an authority table, after the commit's last refusal and before its first publication any other task can see (ATTACK-4 B1). That deletes ATTACK-3's durable-before-fence interval.
- **v2.17 against both.** Per-session roots order a writer against its *own* session's fence (B1), need an initial authority per join (M4), a failed-watch read set (M2), roots and sentinels in every ledger (M3), and prior digests to soften grows. (a) needs one register, a `Latest` root (ATTACK-4 B2).

### Release order: the design

1. **Authority tables (B2).** `policy(r, f, reads, n, s)` declares the table names the policy may read. The policy is `StaticFn[fn(Tx, Caller) -> Result[KeyScope, Capacity]]` (ATTACK-4 B2: the opaque `Auth` is deleted).
   - **Its only inputs are a narrowed view Tx and the Caller.** It gets no `H`, so it cannot reach a `Latest` built at open. `authorize` narrows its Tx read-only to the declared tables, so a read of any other table is `OutOfScope`. **`Tx` is `pub opaque scoped` (PROG-2; VERIFY-5 B2):** outside the store module its fields (`info` with a look's snapshot version, the roots, the scope) cannot be read, so the policy sees the declared tables only through `fetch`, `range` and `lookup`, plus `catalog_table` and the Caller. `store.info(mut tx)` stays the body's way to its CallInfo, and a view cannot call it.
   - **Naming a grant target is not reading it.** `store.catalog_table[K, V](tx, name, key, value) -> Result[Table[K, V], Error]` resolves any table in the Tx's catalog with a handle's codec check (`Missing(name)`, `Schema(i)`), and commits nothing. A `Table` only names: `scope`, `scope_keys`, `none(tx)` and `everything(tx)` build grants from it, and its rows stay unreadable outside the declared tables. So a policy that reads `members` can grant a range of `notes` without making `notes` an authority table.
   - **Checked:** probe `/tmp/cs1128-probe/p5s` (a copy of the worktree's `store.hirsel` with these declarations added), accepted: a `StaticFn[fn(Tx, Caller) -> ..]` policy that fetches from one table through `catalog_table`, grants `scope_keys` over another table named the same way, tests it with `scope_within`, and sends the scope through a channel inside a `send` record; and a `Latest[Seq]` reader made, `dup`ed and `load`ed. Controls: `p5c` (`KeyScope` not `send`) is refused with CONC-2, and `p5sx` and `p5sy` (a type error put in the policy body and in the channel function) are refused, so both bodies are checked. A first try named the function `within` and was refused with LANG-7, since `within` is already a parameter name in the store; so the public name is `scope_within`. The catalog lookup is a stub and nothing was run, so the probe checks signatures and ownership only. The earlier probe `auth` checked only the `StaticFn` shape, and `authc` only PROG-9. **Pass 6 (VERIFY-5 B2), red:** with `Tx` made `pub opaque scoped`, probe `p6c` (an application module reading `tx.info.id.version` from a view `Tx`) is **accepted**, and so is control `p6m` (a field read of a plain `pub opaque` record), while `p6e` (a type error in that module) is refused, so the module is checked. The checker refuses opaque construction (`values.rs:1059`) and patterns (`patterns.rs:239`) but not field reads (`places.rs:428`). So PROG-2's field-read refusal is RA's first step, with `p6c` as its red case; until then B2 holds only on paper.
   - **Open checks.** Each declared name must be a table in the catalog, or open fails with `OpenError::PolicyTable(name)` (§1.1).
   - **Equal `seq`, equal evaluation (VERIFY-5 B3).** The declared tables live in their own tree, which only an authority commit rebuilds (release 2). A policy reads nothing else. Its Tx's fields are opaque (above), and its reads outside the declared tables are `OutOfScope` before any descent. `catalog_table` reads the catalog, and a catalog change on main is an authority commit too. Its steps and bytes follow from its source and values alone (MEM-30), and it runs under its registered grant (`n`, `s`) with one metered path (release 3). So two evaluations for one Caller at one `seq` descend the same objects and give the same result, a stop included, whatever `bound steps` the policy nests. The writer's equality check (release 5) therefore certifies the grant, not only the authority rows. `handles` keeps rule 12.
2. **The authority counter (store hook 1).** The store keeps `seq: u64` with every snapshot of main, in memory only.
   - A main commit whose write set touches a declared table or the catalog (VERIFY-5 B3: the bump, exactly, below) increments it, and so does every `adopt` (it replaces main's roots wholesale). A commit on any other branch never changes it: the register protects main, the only branch the publisher releases (ATTACK-4 M1).
   - **The authority tree (VERIFY-5 B3).** Every snapshot of main also carries `authority`, an in-memory tree holding a copy of every row and index entry of the declared tables, keyed as in the data tree. `declare_authority` builds it from the data tree at open, and an authority commit rebuilds it with that commit's writes to the declared tables. The policy's `fetch`, `range` and `lookup` read it. Bodies read and write the data tree as before. The tree never reaches disk: `seq` and stamps never cross an open, so neither the format, cuts nor replay change. The cost is one more root per snapshot, admitted with the commit and charged to the Db, and a second copy of the declared tables' rows. In return, a deeper data tree changes no policy's steps.
   - **The bump, exactly.** An authority commit is a commit on main that replaces main's `authority` or `catalog` root, and every `adopt`. A cut or a rotation shares the roots it has, so it is not one.
   - **The register is a `Latest[Seq]` root** (CONC-13; v1 has no atomics, CONC-12), made at Db open with `Seq{v: 0}` and charged to the Db (32 B root plus the `Arc`).
   - **Admission before publication (ATTACK-4 B1).** An authority commit admits its next `Arc[Seq]` before its last refusal point (`run` step 3; `adopt` before its cut). A failed admission refuses the commit with `Refused(c)`, which is `ServeError`-free and leaves everything as it was.
   - **Publication order.** In `publish(db, main)`, the store `latest.publish`es the register's new `Arc` first, and only then the followed snapshot's. That is the commit's first publication another task can observe. A memory store does the same, since the order lives in `publish`, not in the journal. So any task that observes b's roots, through a followed snapshot, a Release or a reply, observes them after the register holds `seq(b)` (CONC-10's one order).
   - **The reader.** `store.AuthorityReader` is `send`, not `shareable`: it wraps one `Latest` handle. `authority_reader(db)` makes the first, `store.dup(r)` (`latest.handle`) makes more and cannot fail, and `store.load(r) -> u64` is one `latest.read`, which neither waits, fails nor admits.
   - At open, `seq` and the register are 0. Every session dies with the process, so no stamp crosses an open.
   - Stored replies keep only their digest (STORE v2.28's 41/73 sizes are unchanged).
3. **Authorize (store hook 2; B3).** `store.authorize(v: Tx, caller, policy, n, s) -> Result[Authorized, Stop]`.
   - It runs the policy in an isolated execution on a snapshot or a Tx view and returns `Authorized{scope: KeyScope, digest: Digest, seq: u64}`.
   - It checks the scope's `StoreId`, empty scopes included. A foreign scope is `Stop::Policy`.
   - `Digest` is plain `{a: u64, b: u64, c: u64, d: u64}`, 32 bytes and `shareable + send`.
   - The head (inside `serve`, and for duplicates), the publisher (G2, G3, rechecks) and one-shot reads all call this one function.
   - **One metered path (ATTACK-4 B3).** On every path, `authorize` gives the policy a fresh, empty read log made inside the policy's byte bound, so its first growth is charged to `n` alike at the head and at the publisher. A read the log cannot record (`Full`) stops the evaluation with `Stop::Policy`: the policy never sees that error, so it cannot answer around an unrecorded dependency. The same holds for any capacity failure of a read.
   - **Reads go into the subscription's read set.** Inside a `look`, on success, `authorize` appends that log to the look's one read log, the single-log path of STORE AJ1 and AK5. So a subscription's read set holds its policy's reads. At the head the log is dropped.
   - **Containment.** `store.scope_within(outer: KeyScope, inner: KeyScope) -> bool` is the store's existing `narrow` relation, now public (ATTACK-4 M5): both scopes name this store, and every inner entry lies inside one outer entry of the same table with equal or greater access. It is conservative, so a grow that re-cuts intervals fails it: a run under `[0,10)` is not within `[0,5) ∪ [5,11)`, and its reply is `Withheld`. A grow that keeps each old entry inside one new entry passes.
4. **The stamp.** Every unit a writer may release carries `stamp`: the `seq` its authorization ran at. Each kind of unit gets its stamp as follows.
   - **Content** (rows, `complete`, a query's data-bearing `Ended`): the base's `seq`.
   - **A `Told` of a run:** the `seq` the run's policy was authorized at, which `serve` returns in the `Receipt` and keeps in the `Pending` through `conclude` (STORE AM3, ATTACK-4 B2). It is never read from the register after the commit: a call that shrinks its own scope commits at a higher `seq`, and its reply must meet the recheck. The `Told` also carries the run's Caller and its scope (in memory, ≤ the policy's `n`). `KeyScope` is `send` (STORE §1.4), so the scope crosses `Release` and the publisher's inbox.
   - **`Duplicate(Stored)`:** the head re-authorizes the requesting Caller at its current snapshot. If the digest equals the stored digest, it answers with that evaluation's `seq` as the stamp, and the `Told` carries that evaluation's scope as its witness for a later recheck. Otherwise it answers `Withheld`, payload-free (V3).
   - **Checked rows (ATTACK-4 B6).** Every Outcome of a run whose body started is checked: `Returned`, `Failed(Raised(n))`, `Failed(OutOfBytes(room))` and `Failed(OutOfTime(s))` (which exit a body took depends on data).
   - **Payload-free units carry no stamp and always pass:** `Refused`, `Abandoned`, `InDoubt`, `Withheld`, `Failed(Policy|Gone)`, `Ended(sub, Failed(Policy))`, `Close`, and a `Reset` that carries no checked row. A `Reset` or `Batch` that carries checked rows is checked row by row like any frame.
   - **A `Told` delivered to a session whose Caller differs from the run's** gets no stamp release. It goes straight to the recheck (step 6).
5. **The writer's check (the release point, ruling (ii)).** After the writer takes a unit from its frame channel, and before the unit's first byte, it loads the register once.
   - Every stamp in the unit ≥ R (its content's and each checked row's): the unit is accepted and written whole: a WebSocket frame, one SSE event, a B-11 head with its body, or an HTTP call response.
   - Otherwise the writer writes nothing and returns `Stale(unit, R)` to its owner.
   - **Every offer loads (ATTACK-4 B5).** A unit the owner offers again, after a recheck or a rebuild, is a new offer: the writer loads again after taking it. No path writes a restamped unit without that load, the HTTP connection's included (http.md B-8).
   - **One unit-return rule (M3):** a unit goes back to its owner only at `Sent` or `Stale`, never at channel handoff. A send's slot credit is released at `Sent` or at the session's end, and a `Stale` unit keeps it (ATTACK-4 M2).
6. **The owner on `Stale(unit, R)` (M1; ATTACK-4 B4, M1).** The owner splits the unit back into its parts.
   - **Content is never restamped (B4).** `content` is true exactly when the `Stale` unit carried content. In that case the owner drops all of the session's held content, every `Ended` that carries data included. It also drops each later content unit until the `Reset` that this recheck owes arrives. A reply-only `Stale` (`content: false`) drops nothing, and later content is delivered as before, since no `Reset` is owed (VERIFY-5 B4). `Reset`s and other payload-free units go on unchanged.
   - **Owed `Told`s stay owed** with their stale counts, which survive every rebuild of a `Batch` and every re-offer.
   - **The recheck.** The owner posts `Recheck{session, R, content}` to `PubIn` with `try_send`, at most one outstanding per session. A later `Stale` raises R and ORs its `content` into the outstanding request, or into the next one if the answer has already been sent (VERIFY-5 B4). Discard ends only at the `Reset` that answers a `content: true` request. If `PubIn` is full, the session ends at once, retryably: WebSocket 1013, SSE reset and end, HTTP 503 `{"refused": "reset"}`.
   - **The publisher's answer.** If its current base has `seq ≥ R` it answers at once; otherwise it keeps the request, one per session, until such a base arrives, and drops it when the session retires. At that base:
     - if `content`, it drops all of the session's subscriptions and queues `Reset` through the owed path (v2.13), behind every content unit already owed;
     - only then it queues `Reauthorized{seq, scope}`, the session Caller's scope from `store.authorize` at that base. A full owner inbox leaves the answer owed on the ledger, retried on `Drained`.
     - If the policy stops, the answer is `Reauthorized(Stopped)`.
   - **A held `Told`** is offered again with stamp `seq` if `store.scope_within(scope, told.scope)` (release 3). Otherwise it becomes `Withheld`: the recheck completed and the scope no longer covers the run's. On `Reauthorized(Stopped)` the session ends retryably at once, as under the bound below (VERIFY-5 M1). Equal `seq` gives an equal evaluation (release 1), so a second recheck at that `seq` would stop again.
   - **Bound.** A `Told` that comes back `Stale` 3 times ends retryably, never as `Withheld`: on WebSocket the session closes with 1013 and the client re-sends and gets the duplicate path; on HTTP 503 `{"refused": "reset"}` with `Retry-After: 1`. Content has no bound: it is dropped at its first `Stale`.
   - **No progress guarantee under churn.** A hot authority table, even one the Caller's rows never touch, can give three completed stale checks. Reconnecting buys a new attempt, not a delivery.
7. **Subscriptions (option (b), for content).** Each subscription's read set already holds its policy's reads (v2.15), so G2 re-runs it when its authorization might have changed.
   - **New digest:** if the re-run's digest differs from the subscription's last one, the publisher drops all of the session's subscriptions and queues `Reset` owed like `Ended` (v2.13). One Caller has one scope, so a change is session-wide. The client resubscribes and hides its cache until each fresh `complete`, as Convex re-runs every query on an identity change.
   - **Policy failure:** `Ended(sub, Failed(Policy))`, payload-free and final for that subscription (M1).
   - **No session watch:** no read set is kept for a failed policy (M2).
   - G2 is not the content check: a unit that a session already holds is checked only by its writer (release 5), and dropped on `Stale` (release 6).
8. **Joins.** `Joined` carries the Caller and a `dup` of the place's `AuthorityReader` (release 2), and nothing has to be installed before a checked handoff. This deletes M4's race.

**L28's orders, each decided:**

| Order | What happens |
|---|---|
| **visible before fence** | Cannot occur: `publish` stores the register before the followed snapshot's `latest.publish`, b's first observable effect (ATTACK-4 B1). Durability comes later still. |
| **fence before read** | The writer loads `seq(b)`; a unit stamped below it is `Stale`. |
| **read before fence** | The unit was accepted before b's effective point and finishes. b's acknowledgement leaves only after `durable(b)` (V4), which is after the register store. |
| **reload after re-offer** | A unit offered again after a recheck is loaded again (release 5), so a second authority commit during the recheck is caught. |
| **two writers partway** | There is one register and one store, so every load is ordered against it. No order puts A after b and B before b while B loads later. |

**What goes away:**
- per-session roots, `Scope`, `Authority`, `Auth`, the epoch, the sentinel, fence publication and G2 step 0;
- restamping of content (ATTACK-4 B4) and the equal-evaluation claim (B3);
- prior digests, the session watch and its failed-read problem (M2), and the relay join race (M4);
- the owner's once-per-epoch rule.

**What stays:**
- the release point at writer handoff;
- `Withheld` as a terminal answer;
- `Reset` for content;
- B-11's 503 `reset`.

**What it costs a real client:**
- **Any authority write makes every in-flight unit on every session stale.**
  - A stale `Told` costs one recheck round trip, normally with no visible effect. Stale content costs a `Reset` and a resubscribe.
  - A place should declare only the tables its policy reads, and should not use a hot table as an authority table.
  - If authority commits keep arriving faster than one publisher round plus one handoff, `Told`s reach the 3-stale bound and sessions close retryably. Results are not destroyed, but a client gets no guarantee of eventual delivery while the churn lasts.
- **A scope change of the Caller (grow or shrink) resets its subscriptions.** The client resubscribes, as on Convex and Zero.
- **`Withheld` narrows to two cases:**
  - a recheck of an in-flight reply whose run scope is not `store.scope_within` the Caller's current scope (a revocation, a self-shrink, or a grow that re-cuts the run's intervals);
  - a duplicate (reconnect, HTTP retry, K-5 retry) whose stored digest differs from the Caller's current digest. Only the digest is stored, so a grow between the run and the retry also gives `Withheld`.
  - A grow witnessed while the reply is in flight, the call's own grow included, no longer withholds: containment is checked against the run's scope.
- **Memory:** an owed `Told` holds its run's scope until `Sent` (≤ the policy's `n`; M3), and during a recheck the answer's scope lives beside it (≤ `n` more). The register is one `Latest` root and one `Arc[Seq]` per authority commit.

### Interface between the two designs

The interface is built first as its own small slice, **RA**. It holds everything both designs use, with these signatures (ATTACK-4 B2):
1. **Declaration and counter:** `policy(r, f, reads, n, s)`, `OpenError::PolicyTable(name)`, the per-commit flag, `seq` on main's snapshots, and the register published in `publish` before the followed snapshot (STORE AM8).
2. **Authorize:** `store.authorize(v: Tx, caller, policy, n, s) -> Result[Authorized{scope, digest, seq}, Stop]`, with its one metered path (release 3), and `store.scope_within(outer, inner) -> bool`.
3. **Reader:** `AuthorityReader: send`, `store.authority_reader(db)`, `store.dup(r)`, `store.load(r) -> u64`. The caller of `place_head` takes one reader with `authority_reader(db)` before it moves the Db in, and gives each `connect` a `dup` of it.
4. **Policy shape:** `StaticFn[fn(Tx, Caller) -> Result[KeyScope, Capacity]]` over an opaque `Tx` (VERIFY-5 B2), with its `reads` declared at open and held in the authority tree (VERIFY-5 B3), `store.catalog_table` for naming grant targets, `KeyScope: send`, and `serve` taking the policy and running it through `authorize`.
5. **Receipt:** `Committed(Receipt)` and `Kept(Receipt)`, `Receipt{ticket, reply, digest, scope, seq}` (AM3), so a fresh success carries the run's scope, digest and the `seq` its stamp comes from.

RA's law case builds a real policy, serves a call with it, sends the receipt's scope through a channel and loads the reader on the other side. The store-contracts slice (RS) uses 2, 4 and 5. The release slice (RR) uses all five. After RA, RS and RR do not wait on each other.

### ATTACK-3 findings → resolution

| # | Finding | Resolution | Where |
|---|---|---|---|
| B1 | Per-session fences cannot make one durable revocation take effect everywhere at once | (ATTACK-4 B1 moved the store before the followed snapshot's publication.) **One store-owned authority counter, stored inside the revoking commit before its frame is queued, and loaded by every writer after it takes a unit.** All four orders are decided (table above), and L28 now distinguishes them. No acknowledgement wait is needed: a writer that loaded the old value accepted its unit before the effective point. | v2.18 release 2, 5; §3; §8 L28 |
| B2 | Open-only schema does not make the policy a function of tracked state | (ATTACK-4 B2 replaced `Auth` with a narrowed `Tx`.) **The policy is `StaticFn[fn(store.Auth, Caller) -> ..]`, reading only declared tables in an isolated execution under a fixed grant.** No `H`, so no `Latest` built at open. Probe `auth` accepted; control `authc` refused with PROG-9. The counterexample needs the policy to read a value from H, which its type no longer allows. | release 1; §1.1 |
| B3 | The publisher cannot construct the initial grant through the declared interfaces | **`store.authorize` on a snapshot or Tx view, returning a plain 32-byte `Digest` and checking `StoreId`, empty scopes included.** There is no shareable `Authority` holding `Bytes` any more, so OWN-4's missing declaration has nothing to apply to. | release 3; STORE AM8 |
| M1 | Stale content at the current epoch can prevent progress | **Payload-free units carry no stamp and always pass, `Ended(sub, Failed(Policy))` included. A stale unit is rechecked, not reset per epoch, and a unit stale 3 times ends retryably.** There are no epochs. | release 4, 6 |
| M2 | A failed watch has no returned read set | **Deleted with the session watch.** A failed subscription policy ends that subscription; nothing waits on a failed read set. | release 7 |
| M3 | Publisher and relay budgets omit live allocations | (ATTACK-4 B2, M2 corrected the register's cost and rows 16b/16c.) **No per-session roots, sentinels or replacement `Arc`s: one 8-byte register.** Row 16c reserves `DONE_MAX + 8` (stamp) plus the run's scope (≤ the policy's `n`) per owed `Told` until `Sent`. A unit returns to its owner only at `Sent` or `Stale`. | §3 writer; §3 budgets; http.md 16c |
| M4 | A relay join can beat its own initial authority | **Deleted.** There is no initial authority to install. A stamp is compared with the one global register, which every session reads. | release 8 |
| B4 | Keyed recovery loses the authority and reason of its terminal reply | (ATTACK-4 B7: now `NoRoom(Pending, Capacity)`.) `NoRoom(Pending)`: the Pending keeps the Completion, digest, record, CallInfo and stop. K-8's third refusal concludes it once from the claim's reserve, and the body does not run again. | STORE AM1 |
| B5 | Migration rollback has two commit units | **A migration is staged and published in one catalog commit with one checkpoint.** A failure drops the stage, and the old catalog stays. | STORE AM2 |
| B6 | A post-publication error cannot promise Admitted | **An `Error` before the publish point leaves p Admitted. An error at or after the publish point poisons the head (exit), as the store already does** (corrected by ATTACK-4 m1: `After` starts at the publish point, not at journal handoff). The same holds for `conclude`. | STORE AM4 |
| B7 | The adapter's complete charge has no derivation | (ATTACK-4 B8 corrected `CallInfo` to 44 B and derived A_s for the work left outside the bounds.) Bytes: `2·n_p + n_e + A_b`, with each term derived. Steps: `s_p + s_e + A_s`, after moving the reply write and issued-ID finalization inside the body's bound. Checked arithmetic at registration. | STORE AM5 |
| M5 | A fresh success returns only a ticket | `Committed(Receipt)` / `Kept(Receipt)`: `Receipt{ticket, reply, digest, scope}`, first delivery, with no read-back. `conclude` returns the same. | STORE AM3 |
| M6 | A fault's record can replay under the wrong CallInfo | **The Pending keeps the attempted run's CallInfo and base, and `conclude` writes them into the record.** Replay reads CallInfo from the record. | STORE AM1, AM6 |
| M7 | The record omits the selected handles constructor; a program without revisions has no policy revision | (ATTACK-4 M4: the constructors go into a replay identity derived in `place_head`, not into the launch digest.) **The launch digest includes the identity bytes of `handles` and `migrate`.** An absent revision encodes as 32 zero bytes, and recorded-code mode answers `Unknown(RevisionUnknown)` for it. | STORE AM7; §7 |
| M8 | Namespace comparison is incomplete at bind and retirement | **Bind compares the verifier kind with the namespace's identity declaration, and the retired-op comparison includes the stored namespace.** Secret rotation is unchanged. | STORE AM7; http.md K-1 |
| m1 | Two obsolete instructions | **H2 says two isolated executions. STORE §2.4 says a reply adds `32 + R` to the admission frame.** | §2 H2; STORE AM7 |

ATTACK-3's sizes and retry analysis: the 41/73 and K-7 figures stand. The client-cost paragraph replaces the "conditional cost" analysis, because prior digests are gone.

## CHANGES-v2.17 (astra's second attack on v2.16; the orchestrator's candidate release order)

v2.17 answers caller-scope-1128/ATTACK-2.md, which found 6 BLOCKER, 3 MAJOR and 1 MINOR issues in v2.16 at b0ca7398. RC1 (L31, m1, m3, the stream key and the fresh-store cutover) is unchanged and is built by its own lane. v2.17 changes everything after RC1.

It supersedes these v2.16 items:
- B1 (the bell, `Rung`, and the owner's check);
- B2's release check at the head;
- B3's `Schema.migrate`;
- B4's public `reply` and `record`;
- M4's measured constants.

It amends M1, M2 and M3. It is text only. No model was re-run. v2.16 is this file at b0ca7398.

The `serve` shape below was checked by the checker before it was written here. A copy of store.hirsel with `serve`, `conclude` and `untried` beside `stage` was accepted (63 files). `serve` runs two isolated executions in turn, each over a Tx it builds from views of the head's snapshot: the policy's, under `s_p`, which returns the scope and its digest; and the body's, under `s_e`, which takes a `clone` of the scope, moves its Tx into the body's bound and gets it back. Between the two, outside both, `serve` sets the digest in its local `Completion`. These controls were refused:
- setting the digest inside the policy's execution, with MEM-30 "a step bound cannot mutate an outer place";
- a nested `isolate` around the body that takes the Tx mutably, with FAIL-16 "isolate's body may not capture a mutable place";
- both executions inside one outer bound, with MEM-30 "a step bound needs a visible callback descriptor without outer mutation";
- (v2.17's first pass) lending the outer `mut Tx` to the body bound, with MEM-30, and `take c` inside the closure, with LANG-14.
The store already has local names `answer` and `pending`, so the public functions are `conclude` and `untried` (LANG-7).

**Where v2.17 departs from the orchestrator's candidate or from ATTACK-2**, the row says **DEPARTS** and why.

| # | Finding | Decision | What v2.17 says | Basis |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| B1 | A revocation and a writer handoff had no common order | **FIXED: the writer is the serialization point, as the candidate says. DEPARTS in three ways:** a `Latest` root instead of an inbox fence; the publisher fences instead of the head; a reply carries a digest instead of an epoch | **The authority root.** Each session has one `Latest[Authority]` root (CONC-13): a WebSocket session, an SSE group, a one-shot read, and (new) each HTTP call response.<br>• `Authority = {epoch: u64, scope: Granted(digest) \| Unauthorized}`.<br>• The session creates the root when it connects, holding `{0, Unauthorized}`. So **a writer starts unauthorized**. It passes one handle to its writer and one to the publisher in `Joined`.<br>• The root's 32 B are charged to the session's scope. Each `Arc[Authority]` the publisher publishes is charged to the publisher's ledger for that session.<br>• At join the publisher also admits a **sentinel** `Arc` holding `{u64 max, Unauthorized}`. A publish never fails, so if a later `Arc` cannot be admitted the publisher publishes the sentinel instead. A writer that reads the sentinel ends its session: WebSocket close 1013, or HTTP 503 with `Retry-After: 1` before the head.<br>**The fence** is `latest.publish`.<br>**The acceptance point** is the writer's `latest.read`. The writer reads it after it takes a unit from its channel and before it writes the unit's first byte.<br>• Publish and read are events in CONC-10's one order, so each fence and each acceptance are ordered with no channel between them. A fence cannot overflow, the writer drains nothing, and an idle writer needs nothing.<br>• **DEPARTS (the candidate's Fence message in the writer inbox):** an idle writer never drains such a channel, so a second fence would find it full. A `Latest` keeps only the newest value, which is all the check needs.<br>**The units:** a WebSocket frame, one SSE event, and an HTTP response head with its whole body (the one-shot read and the call response). A unit the writer accepted before a fence finishes.<br>**The check,** at acceptance:<br>• subscription content (rows, `complete`, a query's `Ended`) passes only if the unit's epoch equals the root's epoch and the scope is `Granted`;<br>• each **checked** Outcome (item B2) passes only if its stored digest equals the root's digest or its prior;<br>• everything else passes: V1 refusals, `Abandoned`, `Withheld`, compute and granted replies, and the payload-free faults.<br>**If the check fails**, the writer writes nothing and reports `Stale(epoch, scope)` to the owner in place of `Sent`. The owner then:<br>• drops every unsent piece of subscription content whose epoch is older;<br>• replaces each failing checked Outcome with `Withheld`;<br>• queues `Reset(version, outcomes)`, once per epoch;<br>• hands the rebuilt frame over.<br>An owed Outcome stays owed until its row is `Sent`. A unit holds the slot units of its rows until then (§3).<br>**Who fences: DEPARTS (the candidate has the head fence in the same head turn).** The publisher fences when its base advances. Before it does any other act at base b, it re-runs every session watch that a change in (a, b] hits, and publishes each changed `Authority`. The other acts are: G2 content, a G3 for a `Subscribed` taken after that Release, a V3 delivery, a `Told` to an owner or to the HTTP relay, and the Batches.<br>**Why the publisher:** a session's watch is its policy's read set in the interval index (§4). Fencing at the head would put every session's index lookup and policy re-run on the head's write path. The head holds no read sets.<br>**The invariant, path by path:** every effect of b that a client can see comes after b's fences are published.<br>• Every Outcome the head decides (V3, V4, V5), the revoker's included, reaches an owner or the relay only as the publisher's `Told`. The relay never takes an answer directly from the head (http.md B-8).<br>• **A `Told` leaves only at a base ≥ the version of every `Released` the publisher took before it.** A snapshot-less Release (V3) waits for the round, fences first, of every snapshot Release ahead of it in `PubIn`. So an answer that reports no new commit, such as the revoker's re-send of its own position answered `Duplicate`, also leaves after b's fences. An answer that reports b's position is decided only after b's worker ended, and that worker pushed b's Release first (H3, V4), so FIFO order puts b's Release ahead of it.<br>• Every row, `complete` and `Ended` at a version ≥ b is made at base ≥ b (G2, G3).<br>• SSE and B-11 content travels the same way.<br>• So the revoker's acknowledgement leaves the publisher after the victim's fence. A victim writer either read its root before that fence (it accepted earlier, which is allowed) or reads the new value.<br>**The cost, stated:** the window in which b is durable but not yet fenced lasts until the publisher's base reaches b. That is one publisher round behind the head, not one head turn. In that window nobody can observe b, so nothing is acknowledged before it is enforced.<br>**The epoch.** The publisher increases a session's epoch when the watch's new scope does not contain the old one (by `narrow`'s containment rule), or when the watch fails. A scope that only grew publishes the same epoch with the new digest, and the digest it replaced becomes the prior (B2). An increase clears the prior. On an increase the publisher drops the session's subscriptions. It then queues `Reset(epoch)` to the owner like an owed `Ended`: it is held on the session's ledger while the inbox is full and retried on `Drained` (v2.13). So a quiet session still learns of it. The client resubscribes, and each sub completes afresh (§4 "Resume").<br>**G3 under a new epoch:** G3 runs at the publisher's base. Its content is stamped with the epoch current at that base, so a first `complete` made before a revocation is stale at the writer.<br>**Watch failure.** A watch that faults or runs out publishes `{e+1, Unauthorized}`. The partial read set is kept. The watch is deterministic, so it re-runs, and may recover, only when one of those reads changes. A watch the publisher cannot admit (no reservation on the ledger) publishes the sentinel, so the session ends.<br>**A `Joined` that does not fit** (`PubIn` full) means the session never authorizes: WebSocket closes with 1013, and an HTTP request gets 503 with `Retry-After: 1` before its forward.<br>**Crash.** If the process dies after b is durable and before the publisher's base reaches b, every session, relay and HTTP connection dies with it. Clients reconnect and join under the new state, whose first base is ≥ b.<br>**Coalesced grant-then-revoke, DEPARTS (the candidate gives the intermediate reply its own epoch):** a reply carries the **digest** of the scope it ran under, and the writer compares it with the root's digest. Suppose one coalesced group grants, a call runs under the grant (digest D_big), and the grant is revoked. The watch sees D_small at both ends and publishes nothing, but D_big ≠ D_small, so the reply is `Withheld`. An epoch would need numbering per Caller at the head, and the head has no session state. | orchestrator's candidate; CONC-10; CONC-13; tasks.md CONC-12; http.md B-8 |
| B2 | Release used the head's newest snapshot; a coalesced grant passed; fault details and store identity were open | **FIXED** | **Checked outcomes.** Every Outcome of a run whose body started is checked, whatever its exit, because which exit a body took depends on data: `Returned(Ok)`, `Returned(Error)`, `Failed(Raised(n))`, `Failed(OutOfBytes(room))` (the length of the oversize reply), and `Failed(OutOfTime(s))`. Each is stored with the run's scope digest. **Payload-free outcomes:** `Failed(Policy)` (new, `Fault` tag 4), `Failed(Gone)`, every `Refused`, `Abandoned` and `Withheld` carry no application data, are stored with the digest of `none`, and always pass. A failed policy is answered with the fixed `Failed(Policy)` and none of its fault details, so nothing the policy read leaks through the fault. **The release decision is the writer's** (B1). The head checks no policy at release. It returns `Duplicate(Stored{reply, scope_digest})`, the store's saved digest, for stream duplicates and keyed duplicates alike (http.md K-5). The writer compares that digest with its root, which reflects the durable state at the publisher's base, not the head's newest snapshot. **A failed release-time watch** leaves the root `Unauthorized`, so every checked Outcome is `Withheld`, even one whose original run had legitimately run under an empty scope. **Locality (ATTACK-2):** the digest input begins with the `StoreId` (STORE v2.28 AL3). A policy result whose scope names another store is `Foreign`. A watch that gets one fails (`Unauthorized`), and a `serve` that gets one fails the policy (`Failed(Policy)`). **Digest equality, with the epoch's prior digest.** After a pure grow the root holds the new digest and the one it replaced in the same epoch, `Granted{digest, prior}`. A checked Outcome passes when its digest equals either. That is sound: within one epoch each published scope contains the one before it, so the prior scope lies inside the current one. So a call that grows its own caller's scope (join a room, create with membership) gets its own reply. These give `Withheld`: equal rights under a different encoding (one interval against two adjacent ones), a scope two grows back, a scope never published (coalesced), and any shrink. That fits the completion contract: `Withheld` is final, the client never repeats the effect, and it re-reads through a query. **The cost, stated:** a call that shrinks its own caller's scope (leave a room) gets `Withheld` for its own reply, and a reply that waits through two grows does too. The prior lives only in the root of a session that joined before the grow, so a WebSocket reconnect, an HTTP retry and a K-5 keyed retry join with `prior: None` and get `Withheld` (403) for a reply that ran before the grow. | ATTACK-2 B2; STORE v2.28 AL3 |
| B3 | `migrate` in `Schema` let H keep a bit derived from row data | **FIXED, as ATTACK-2 proposes** | **Two phases.** `pub fn migrate(mut m: store.Migration) -> Result[(), store.Error] waits` is a separate function, given to `place_head` beside `handles`. It creates tables and indexes and migrates rows. The runner runs it once per open, in its own transaction, before `handles`. It returns nothing that reaches H. `handles(s: store.Schema) -> Result[H, store.Error]` then only **opens** existing tables and indexes. `Schema` has no create, migrate, snapshot, look or row operation. A name it cannot open fails the open with `Store(Missing)`. **Fresh store:** the first open's `migrate` creates every table and index. So `handles` never meets an empty catalog for a name the place uses. **A failed migration** (fault, exhaustion, `Error`, or a duplicate key after a transform) rolls back its transaction. The open fails with `OpenError.Migrate(store.Error)`, and the store stays at its previous catalog. **H is a function of the catalog alone**, and only `migrate` changes the catalog. The head runs `handles` twice on the same catalog, which gives H's with the same incarnations, so v2.16's `HandlesChanged` check and law L14 are deleted: an open-only `Schema` cannot commit. | rule 12; STORE v2.28 AL4 |
| B4 + new | A Completion could not be consumed inside the head's callback, and recovery had no Completion | **FIXED: the store owns the sequence (STORE v2.28 AL1)** | `store.serve(mut db, take c: Completion, plan: Plan, take rec: Option[CallRecord], policy, body) -> Result[Served, Error] waits` is the only way to run an admitted position, and `store.conclude` the only way to answer one without running it. Every admission that can run gives a Completion: `Admitted(c)`, `Abandoned(c)` (a position from a crashed open, H4), and the keyed claims' equivalents. `Duplicate(Stored)` gives none. **`serve` runs two isolated executions over one snapshot:**<br>1. **The policy's**, under `s_p` and `n_p`: it builds a Tx from views of the head's snapshot, runs the policy, and computes the scope's digest. It returns the scope and the digest.<br>2. **Between them**, outside both, `serve` sets that digest in its local Completion. So the digest survives any later exit, a raise included.<br>3. **The body's**, under `s_e` and `n_e`: it builds a second Tx over the same snapshot, narrowed to a `clone` of the scope (one core call; its bytes ≤ `n_p`). It moves the Tx into the body's bound and gets it back on every normal exit (an exhausted body drops it, which rolls it back), and writes the reply from a view of the Completion. A raise is caught by this execution's `isolate`.<br>**One snapshot:** `serve` holds `mut db` throughout, and the head is the only writer, so nothing commits between the two executions. The policy and the body see the same version, as L26's `policy_before_run` requires.<br>The Completion is consumed only outside the closures. The record is attached, and the frame encoded and committed, after the body's execution, in the reply's transaction. So record and reply commit together or not at all, and a reply that cannot be written drops the record with it. **`Served = Committed(Ticket) \| Kept(Ticket) \| Failed(Pending, Stop) \| NoRoom`**, with `Stop = Raised(u32) \| OutOfSteps(u64) \| OutOfBytes(Capacity) \| Policy`. A `Pending` (opaque, not copy, codec or send) holds the Completion with its digest and the record. The runner maps each exit one to one:<br>• `Committed` is `Returned(Ok)` and `Kept` is `Returned(Error)`;<br>• `Failed(p, Raised(n))` is `Failed(Raised(n))`, `Failed(p, OutOfSteps(s))` is `Failed(OutOfTime(s))`, and `Failed(p, OutOfBytes(c))` is `Failed(OutOfBytes(room))`, for a bound's byte exhaustion and for a reply longer than `plan.reply_max` alike. These three carry the run's digest and are checked;<br>• `Failed(p, Policy)` (a policy that faulted, ran out or returned a `Foreign` scope) carries `none`'s digest and is `Failed(Policy)`.<br>**`serve` returns `Result[Served, Error] waits`**, with `Error` for a store failure, after which the position stays `Admitted`. The runner answers a `Pending` with `store.conclude(mut db, take p: Pending, take r: Bytes) -> Result[Served, Error] waits`. It writes the fixed fault's bytes with the Pending's digest and its record in one streams-only commit, and runs no code. A gone key turns `Admitted(c)` into `store.untried(take c, take rec)`, which carries `none`'s digest, and answers it `Failed(Gone)` (V5). **`NoRoom`:** the store refused the commit (resident limit). The Tx, the record and the Completion are dropped and nothing is stored, so the stream stays `Admitted(p)`. The answer is `Refused(Overloaded)` as V3, and P1 lets the client re-send p, which gets `Abandoned(c)`. `conclude` gives `Committed` or `NoRoom`. The public `reply` and `record` are deleted. A body never holds a Completion or a Pending. | ATTACK-2 "Completion ownership"; LANG-14; MEM-30; checked probe |
| M1 | Recorded-code mode could match without identifying `handles` | **FIXED** | **Recorded-code mode** requires a recorded launch digest that is non-empty and equal to the running launch's, and equal `authority` bytes. Otherwise the verdict is `Unknown(RevisionUnknown)`. The launch digest covers `place`, `migrate` and `handles`, because they are in the one image. **Candidate mode** always runs, and the differences show as `Different`. Replay step 3 now says so (§7). **`authority` gets a place in the CALL section** (STORE v2.28 AL5): `u32 length ‖ bytes` after `revision`. | PROG-9; STORE v2.28 AL5 |
| M2 | K-7 kept 525, and 41 against 73 was not carried through | **FIXED** | A `Replied` value now holds the 32 B digest, so its header is **73 B**. An `Admitted` header stays **41 B**. These change: stream rows (73 + R), pack leaves (49 + S Admitted, 81 + S + R Replied), the commit example (273 → 305 from the digest, and 373 + L with AL5's authority, L the launch digest's length), L29's figure (STORE v2.28 AL6), and K-7:<br>• the claims value is 73 + `DONE_MAX`;<br>• the claim reservation is max(41 + `arg_max`, 73 + `DONE_MAX`);<br>• `claim_pexp` is E(526, 0), the 525 is gone;<br>• the count entry is E(131, 8);<br>• `k_phys` ≤ 537.<br>The stale "principal ≤ 64 B" text is deleted (http.md K-7). | http.md K-4, K-7; STORE v2.28 AL6 |
| M3 | Two verifiers could declare the same namespace | **FIXED, as ATTACK-2 proposes** | A namespace is owned place-wide. At most one live verifier declaration may name it, and a second is `RegisterError.NamespaceTwice`. The store's `namespaces` tree records each namespace's verifier kind and its sender-name rule at first admission, and keeps the entry after its op is retired. A later declaration that differs is `OpenError.NamespaceChanged`. Two ops share a namespace only by naming the same verifier declaration, so they are one identity domain. The largest subject is 1 + 24 + 1 + 32 = 58 B ≤ `SUBJECT_MAX` (http.md K-1, K-2, K-4). | http.md K-1, K-2 |
| M4 | The adapter's bounds were measured constants, and the outer `mut Tx` could not be lent (MEM-30) | **FIXED** | **Each Tx is local to its isolated execution** (B4 above), so MEM-30's refusal no longer applies. On each exit:<br>• the body's normal exit hands the Tx back;<br>• exhaustion drops it, rolling back;<br>• the policy's Tx ends with the policy's execution;<br>• a fault in `isolate` drops it.<br>**Steps:** the head reserves `s_p + s_e + ADAPTER_STEPS` once. The policy's execution is bounded by `s_p` and the body's by `s_e`; they cannot share one outer bound (MEM-30, header). `ADAPTER_STEPS` is counted from `serve`'s source: a core call is one step (MEM-30), and `serve` makes a fixed number of calls and has no loops outside the two executions. **Variable work goes to a named allowance:** the scope's digest, its sorting, its encoding and the containment check run inside the policy's own bound. So they are paid from `s_p` and `n_p`, and a many-entry policy pays for its own entries. **Bytes:** the head reserves `2·n_p + n_e + ADAPTER_BYTES`. At body entry the live charge is the returned scope and its clone, 2L ≤ `2·n_p`, plus fixed adapter state. After the body it is 2L + the reply ≤ `2·n_p + n_e`. Frame encoding and commit come after both executions, under the head's own `frame_max`. **Overflow:** registration refuses a sum that overflows u64 with `RegisterError.Bounds`. **The read log:** `serve` keeps none, because the head is the only writer and no transact result uses a read set. A `look`'s log is the read set. Its growth is charged to the publisher's ledger for the session (MEM-26: the log is created outside the bound), and an append over the read-set limit ends that subscription with `Ended(sub, Refused(Budget))`. **The watch** (B1) is charged to the session's `budget` ledger: its retained scope (≤ `n_p`), its read set, and its containment check. A watch that cannot be admitted publishes the sentinel and ends the session. | MEM-25; MEM-26; MEM-30; ATTACK-2 M4 |
| HTTP | A call response had no caller-specific release; `Withheld` had no status | **FIXED** | **Each HTTP response with checked bytes is released at its own writer.** A connection that will write a call response or a B-11 read first joins the publisher as a marked session with its Caller and an authority handle. Its `Joined` is enqueued in `PubIn` before its Ask, so its fence is in place before any `Told` for its send. The connection leaves after the response, as B-11 reads do. The publisher holds at most `streams_max + reads_max + http_max + 1` sessions (http.md §2, HTTP-15 row 14b). **B-7:** `Withheld` is 403 `{"outcome": "withheld"}`. It is terminal, and the generated client never retries it. `Refused(NotAllowed)`'s 403 has no body, so the two are told apart by the body. `Failed(Policy)` is 500 `{"fault": "policy"}`. **K-5:** a matching claim gives the original status and body only if the writer's check passes, otherwise `Withheld`'s 403. **B-11:** a read whose check fails before its head is answered 503 `{"refused": "reset"}` with `Retry-After: 1`, and the client re-requests. A head that passed is written with its body as one unit. | http.md B-7, B-8, B-11, K-5 |
| m2 | L28 could not tell writer acceptance from durability | **FIXED** | L28 controls the two separately. A gate in the test's transport provider decides when the victim's writer may take its next unit. A held sync decides when b becomes durable. The case covers the gap before the fence (§8). | — |
| RC1, L31, m1, m3 | — | **UNCHANGED** | Built by lane caller-rc1-1145 as v2.16 states. | — |

## CHANGES-v2.16 (astra's attack on v2.15; the orchestrator's release ruling)

v2.16 answers caller-scope-1128/ATTACK.md, which found 4 BLOCKER, 5 MAJOR and 3 MINOR issues in v2.15, and it applies the orchestrator's ruling on release authority. **The ruling, logged by the orchestrator, applying FOR-SAM 73:** release authority is (ii). Writer handoff is the release point, ordered against durable authority changes. Unsent subscription content whose authority is stale is discarded. The client then gets a Reset and a fresh `complete`, so every complete Batch still describes one real snapshot. The same applies to G3, WebSocket, SSE and the one-shot HTTP response. v2.16 supersedes v2.15 items 6 (its reading (i) and its `Ended`-before-Batch order) and 9, and it amends items 1, 2, 3, 4 and 7. It is text only, and no model was re-run. v2.15 is this file at ed569cbd. A second astra attack reads v2.16 before Sol builds RC1–RC4. **Where v2.16 departs from ATTACK.md**, the row says **DEPARTS** and why.

| # | Finding | Decision | What v2.16 says | Basis |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| B1 | A revocation and a writer handoff had no common order | **FIXED: the authority bell and the session watch. DEPARTS in how the order is made** | **The release point is writer handoff.** No subscription content is handed to a writer unless its authority is still current at that moment. **The session watch:** every session that releases subscription content or reply bytes joins the publisher when it is admitted (WebSocket at Hello; an HTTP group, SSE stream or one-shot read when it opens; v2.12's join at first Subscribe is replaced). With its `Join` the publisher runs the place's policy for the session's Caller in a `look` at its base and keeps the policy's reads in the interval index as a **watch**: a subscription with no Cover. The watch's charge is on the session's `budget` ledger (M4). **The bell:** each session has a `ch(1, ())` bell. The owner holds the `Receiver`, and the publisher holds the `Sender` in its `Joiner`. In round b, the publisher first re-runs every hit watch at b. If the new scope does not contain the old one, it **rings** that session's bell by `try_send` (a full bell is already rung, so the ring is never lost and the publisher never waits). Then it drops all that session's subscriptions, as a Reset does. Only after every ring of round b does it send any content of round b. A scope that only grew rings nothing, and the watch keeps the new scope. **The owner's check:** immediately before each handoff to the writer, the owner `try_receive`s its bell. If rung: (1) it discards every unsent row and `complete` of every subscription; (2) it replaces the bytes of every unsent `Returned` row with `Withheld` (B2); (3) it queues `Reset(version, outcomes)` as a control frame. The client resubscribes and hides its old cache until each new `complete` or `Ended` (§4, "Resume"). **Why this orders handoff against the durable change (the departure):** ATTACK says that a check made only on notice races while the release sits in `PubIn`, the inbox or the ledger. Commit b becomes observable to any party only through the publisher's round b. The revoker's own Outcome reports a commit, so by rule 7 and V4 it travels in b's Release. Every frame at version ≥ b is made in round b or later. So a handoff before round b's ring is ordered before every observation of b, and a handoff after it sees the ring. The bell is the order point, so no added order to the head is needed. What a writer had already accepted before the ring finishes, as ATTACK allows. **Transports:** a WebSocket owner, an SSE group's owner and a one-shot read's task all check the bell before each handoff. A one-shot read whose bell rings before its response head is handed off discards the frame and answers B-9's 503, so the client re-requests (http.md B-11). An SSE stream writes `event: reset` and ends (http.md §3). **Wake:** after a ring, the publisher also `try_send`s a content-free `Rung` to the owner's inbox. The owner checks its bell after every event it takes as well as before each handoff, so a quiet session resets without other traffic. A dropped `Rung` is safe, because a full inbox has events the owner will take. | orchestrator's ruling; FOR-SAM 73; rule 7; V4 |
| B2 | Dedup released an opaque reply after its read authority had expired | **FIXED, as ATTACK proposes, with a scope digest** | **Completion and disclosure are separate.** A new outcome, `Withheld`, is appended to `Outcome` (tag 5). It means: this position completed, its reply is kept, and its bytes are not released to this caller now. It is final. The client does not retry it, and it may re-read the effect through a query. **The digest.** In the same Tx as its reply, the adapter stores `scope_digest`: the SHA-256 of the scope the policy returned for that run, over its canonical entry encoding (STORE v2.27 AK1). The digest is stored with every reply the runner writes: a commit, the kept reply of an `Error(E)`, and a reply-only `Failed`. **The release check, for every reply with bytes:** an original send or a joined send answered from the commit's own Release is released as `Returned`, because its policy ran in that transaction, and the bell covers its time in the queue. A **duplicate** (H2; a re-send after a reconnect, on either transport), a keyed duplicate (K-5), and an answer from an earlier open's state are all answered by the head. The head runs the policy for the request's Caller in a `look` at its newest snapshot. It answers `Returned(b)` only if that scope's digest equals the stored one, and `Withheld` otherwise. Equality is stricter than containment: a caller whose scope grew gets `Withheld` for an older reply. That is sound, and it costs 32 B per reply instead of a stored scope. `Failed(f)`, `Refused(r)`, `Abandoned` and `Failed(Gone)` carry no application bytes and are never withheld. `Error(E)` travels as `Returned` bytes, so it is checked. **No second run:** `Withheld` never re-runs the entry. Dedup identity (position and request digest) is unchanged. | H2; http.md B-6, K-5 |
| B3 | A second source of store data in H broke the read-set and replay proofs | **FIXED, as ATTACK proposes** | Rule 12 changes: `handles(mut s: store.Schema) -> Result[H, store.Error] waits`. `Schema` (STORE v2.27 AK2) offers only schema operations: make or open a table or index, and migrate. It offers no `snapshot`, `look`, `run` or row read, and it refuses names in the reserved `hirsel.` namespace. So H holds only handles and constants. Every row a policy or a served body sees, an absent row included, is read through the Tx the adapter gives it, and so it is in that run's read set. Re-execution rebuilds the same H at v−1, because H depends on the schema alone. v2.15 item 9 (the author's grant) is withdrawn: there is no such grant now. | rule 12; §7 |
| B4 | A narrowed `mut Tx` could still complete another request's position | **FIXED, as ATTACK proposes** | Reply and record authority stay with the adapter. `store.admit` returns, for each `Admitted` position, an opaque, non-copy, non-codec `Completion` (STORE v2.27 AK3). `record` views it and comes first; `reply` and the keyed claim's reply take it by `take` and come last. So each position is completed exactly once and only by its holder. When the policy fails, the adapter sets the `none` scope's digest on the `Completion` before its reply-only `Failed`, which carries no bytes and is never withheld. The adapter keeps the `Completion` and never passes it to the body, the policy or H. With no `Completion`, a body cannot name a stream or claim position. A body's `info(mut tx)` is still the call's own CallInfo. | store.hirsel:1395; runner.hirsel:1030 |
| M1 | The entry revision alone did not identify the policy for replay | **FIXED, as ATTACK proposes** | `CallRecord` gains `authority: Bytes` (STORE v2.27 AK4): `code.revision(policy) ‖ u64 policy n ‖ u64 policy s ‖ u64 entry n ‖ u64 entry s`, plus the launch digest when it is available (rule 10). Re-execution "under the recorded code" requires the candidate's registration to give the same `authority` bytes. Otherwise the verdict is `Unknown(RevisionUnknown)`. A candidate chosen explicitly still runs, and its policy differences show as `Different`. | §7; STORE §1.8 `revision` |
| M2 | A keyed record has a different grammar, and K-7's reserve was one byte short | **FIXED, as ATTACK proposes** | The Caller is decoded **by the record's control kind**: a stream record as `caller_key ‖ u32-BE(stream)`, and a keyed claim as K-4's `len8 ‖ op ‖ caller_key ‖ len8 ‖ key`. Ownerless keyed recovery (K-8) carries the Caller decoded from the claim key, and it runs the policy with it. L30 covers a keyed record as well as a stream record. http.md K-7's terminal reserve uses E(526, 0) for `claim_pexp`, whose key nests the caller key twice: 65 + 66 + 8 + 387 = 526. | http.md K-4, K-7, K-8 |
| M3 | A webhook sender name is local to its verifier, but it became a subject shared across the place | **FIXED, as ATTACK's second option** | A signed sender's subject is `len8 ‖ namespace ‖ len8 ‖ sender`. `namespace` (≤ 24 B) is a stable, non-secret name the place declares on the verifier (http.md K-2). It is kept in the op record (K-1), so changing it is `KeyedOpChanged`. A route move or a secret rotation keeps it. Two providers that each name a sender `build` therefore have two subjects. The signed-sender issuer still takes its number when Part B's spec lands. | http.md K-1, K-2 |
| M4 | The policy's allowance could be impossible to enter | **FIXED: one combined reservation** | **Who reserves:** the adapter, once per run: the head for a transact call, and the publisher for each G3 run, G2 re-run and watch run. It enters `run` or `look` with `steps = s_policy + s_entry + ADAPTER_STEPS` and `bytes n_policy + n_entry + ADAPTER_BYTES`. `ADAPTER_STEPS` and `ADAPTER_BYTES` are fixed by package `store` (measured in RC2) and cover setup, the digest, encoding and rollback. A watch reserves `s_policy + ADAPTER_STEPS` and `n_policy + ADAPTER_BYTES`. Inside, the policy runs under `bound steps s_policy` and `bound bytes n_policy`, and the body under `s_entry` and `n_entry`. By MEM-30 each inner reservation is taken from what the outer one has left, so every allowance is entered whole. **The returned scope** lives in the outer reservation until the body ends. **Wire mapping:** an outer reservation the head or publisher cannot grant is `Refused(Budget(Room))`, which records nothing. The policy's own exhaustion is `Failed(OutOfTime(s_policy))` or `Failed(OutOfBytes(..))`, recorded reply-only for transact, and `Ended(sub, Failed(..))` for a query. A watch's exhaustion rings the bell (fail closed). **Session charges:** the Points, Spans and interval-index entries kept for each subscription's policy reads and for the watch go on the session's `budget` ledger, beside its read-set bytes (§3). | budgets.md MEM-30 (:244); runner.hirsel:491, :1030 |
| M5 | A clean store could coexist with a replay tape that holds credentials | **FIXED, as ATTACK proposes** | L31 is scoped to durable identity fields the runner generates: stream keys, claim keys, call records, reply rows, and checkpoints of these. **Tapes:** byte-exact provider tapes (Net, Random) are **credential-bearing**. Their files are readable only by the owner, and an export needs an explicit credentials flag (http.md §5). Redacting a tape in place would break replay, so it is never done. **Logs:** routine logs and diagnostics name a session by its Caller. They never include Hello or Welcome bodies, token headers or Random deliveries. | http.md §5 |
| m1 | The tag byte was 22, but the tag is 21 bytes | **FIXED** | The leading byte is the tag's length, 21. Subject = first 16 B of SHA-256(`u8 21 ‖ "hirsel.caller.anon.v1" ‖ token`). **Test vectors** (RUN here with Python's hashlib; RC1 pins them for both ingresses and replay tooling): token `anon:` ‖ bytes 00..0f gives `af66437ced7d1015a113bb47156b71f4`; token `anon:` ‖ sixteen ff bytes gives `4688bb3b3ea1c4af8032c3a1f722776b`. | — |
| m2 | L29 could not show scope parity, and L28 could not observe its order | **FIXED** | L29 starts at p = 0, keeps the duplicate assertion, and adds a fresh call that depends on the Caller, plus two callers' disjoint rows read through WebSocket, SSE and a B-11 read. In L28 only the policy reads the grant, and the case queues an initial `complete` behind a stalled writer when the revocation commits. | §8 |
| m3 | The old-store cutover could repeat an effect | **FIXED, as ATTACK's first option** | RC1 requires a fresh store and a fresh client generation: the SDK's recovery generation is bumped, so a client drops pending state from before RC1. No v2.14 store or client state is kept, so no migration is designed. | — |
| S1 | Slice decision 1: per place | **TAKEN as astra states** | The place-wide policy is the mandatory upper bound, and a body narrows further by argument. **Private existence:** under a partial scope, `lookup` returns `OutOfScope` at the first inaccessible row (AE4 c). That tells an empty result apart from a hidden match. A place whose row existence is private partitions such an index by caller, so a body's lookup range lies inside its scope. | STORE AE4 c |
| S2 | Decision 2: release | **TAKEN** | B1. | — |
| S3 | Decision 3: view feasibility | **TAKEN** | If a nested view cannot share `look`'s log, `look` itself runs the policy and then the scoped body, on its one snapshot and one log (STORE v2.26 AJ1 as corrected by v2.27 AK5). A scope precomputed by a separate `look` is not allowed: it would lose the policy's reads. **Misses and empty ranges are reads:** a `fetch` that finds nothing records its Point, and an empty `range` records its Span (STORE I1). L28's ban-row variant checks this. | store.hirsel:4268 |
| S4 | Decision 4: hot reads | **TAKEN** | A range read shared by N subscriptions' policies costs up to N policy and query runs in each round that hits it. Coalescing lowers the average, but per-subscription limits do not bound a round. A place should give caller-specific grants as caller-specific Point reads. A re-run or watch that runs out resets its session, by the bell, before any data is handed off. | §4 cost |
| S5, S6 | Decisions 5 and 6 | **TAKEN** | B3 and m3. | — |
| S7 | Decision 7: size | **TAKEN** | The anonymous subject stays 16 B at this rung. For N independently minted tokens, the chance of a collision is about N(N−1)/2¹²⁹. The per-IP mint rate bounds neither the total number of identities nor offline inputs. | ingress.hirsel:125 |

## CHANGES-v2.15 (a caller and its key scope; GAPS.md gap 3, lane caller-scope-1128)

Before v2.15, nothing bound a key scope to the admitted caller or to a subscriber's Cover. Every transact body ran in the store's outermost scope (everything, ReadWrite). Every query ran inside `look`, which also sees everything. The principal reached the runner only as the prefix of a stream key, and that prefix was the bearer secret itself: `anon:` plus 16 random bytes, persisted in the streams tree and in every `CallRecord.stream`. v2.15 adds a library-owned `Caller`, one policy per place that turns a Caller into a `KeyScope`, and one adapter that runs that policy inside the same transaction or `look` as the body and then narrows the body to its result. It extends three standing rulings and does not reopen them:
- FOR-SAM 73: "a place's code sees only the caller's principal and its own entries", and "authorization is re-checked before queued data is released";
- ENDSTATE: "authorization is entry code with tracked reads plus narrowing; there is no rule language";
- D4 (DECISIONS): rung 1 is anonymous only, and accounts come later.

Text only. No model has a principal, so no model was re-run. v2.14 is this file at 8f15cd57. Items marked **ATTACK** are the ones this lane is least sure of; astra's attack reads them before any build.

| # | Item | Decision | What v2.15 says | Basis |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The caller value | **NEW. /wholehog: one value names the caller everywhere** (stream key, policy, body, replay) | `pub type Caller: codec + send { issuer: u8, subject: Bytes }`, with `subject` at most `SUBJECT_MAX` = 64 B. It is **not secret**: it may be stored, logged and compared. Its **key** is `caller_key(c) = u8 issuer ‖ u8 len(subject) ‖ subject`, so one key never prefixes another. **Issuer numbers** form a fixed table like `Raised(n)`. Only 1 = anonymous (rung 1) is assigned. A later issuer (a signed keyed-op sender, http.md K-2; an account token, D4) takes the next unused number when its spec lands, and a number is never reused. **Anonymous subject:** the first 16 B of SHA-256(`u8 22 ‖ "hirsel.caller.anon.v1" ‖ token`), where `token` is the 21-byte bearer principal. The hash is one-way, so storing the subject does not store the secret, and the same token always gives the same subject. **The boundary for later issuers:** an issuer maps a verified credential to (issuer number, subject ≤ 64 B, not secret, stable for one account). It never puts a credential in the subject. A token-derived account subject follows SpacetimeDB's and Convex's (issuer, subject) pair, hashed to a bounded length. **The type is plain, not opaque.** Authority comes only from the Caller the runner passes in. A body that builds another Caller value gains nothing, because its scope was already fixed by the runner's Caller. **Compute and granted entries get no Caller.** Compute touches no store. A granted entry's authority over outside operations is its grant G (gap 4). | FOR-SAM 73; DECISIONS D4; SpacetimeDB identity.rs:196-215; Convex broker.rs:543,615 |
| 2 | The stream key | **CHANGED: `caller_key ‖ u32-BE(stream)` replaces `principal ‖ u32-BE(stream)`** | Rule 6, §3 "Frames" and `prepare` use the caller key. Ingress computes the Caller once, at Hello or at the HTTP `Authorization: Bearer` check. `Admitted` carries the `Caller` in place of the principal, and the session keeps only the Caller after Welcome. **No wire change:** a client still sends its token and a u32 stream. Welcome still returns the token. **What it fixes:** the bearer secret no longer reaches the streams tree, the call records, the journal or a checkpoint. **Cutover:** a store written under v2.14 keys holds positions under the old prefix. Rung 1 has no deployed store, so the cutover is clean: no rewrite, and no reading of old keys. A client that reconnects to such a store finds a fresh stream (its p > 0 is `Rejected`, and W4c retires the stream). http.md's K-4 claim key changes the same way: (op, caller key, key) in place of (op, principal, key). That is Part B text, not built. | ingress.hirsel `Principal`, `bind`; runner.hirsel `prepare`; http.md B-6, K-4 |
| 3 | The policy | **NEW: one policy per place, registered explicitly. ATTACK (per place or per entry)** | `pub fn policy[G: send, H: send](mut r: Registry[G, H], f: StaticFn[fn(Tx, H, Caller) -> Result[KeyScope, Capacity]], n: u64, s: u64) -> Result[(), RegisterError]`. **Exactly one per place.** A second registration is `Error(PolicyTwice)`. A place that registers none fails `place_head` with `OpenError.NoPolicy`, so there is **no default**. A public place writes one line: `Ok(store.everything(tx))`. The policy is app code: it may read any table through its view `Tx`, and it runs under its own byte bound `n` and step bound `s`. **It is a function of (Caller, H, the rows it reads) only.** It has no clock or random capability (its type has none), no `info` (a view Tx has none, §1.1), the same H for the whole open (rule 12), and a fixed Caller for each session. This premise is what makes item 6 sound. **Why per place:** this is the one place a rule cannot be forgotten. A place with 40 entries cannot have one entry that skips the check, which is the usual way row authority fails (an object reference anyone can guess). An entry still narrows further by argument, with `store.narrow`, as ENDSTATE already says. **ATTACK:** a per-place policy cannot see the argument. So a user in many rooms gets a scope with one entry per room, and `key_in` checks the entries one by one on every read (store.hirsel:398). The cost is bounded by the policy's `n` and `s`, and by the body's own bounds for each read. A per-entry policy `fn(Tx, H, Caller, A)` gives smaller scopes, but it can be forgotten per entry. | ENDSTATE "entry code with tracked reads plus narrowing"; Convex users.ts (the convention this replaces) |
| 4 | One adapter for both kinds | **NEW** | `authorize(tx: Tx, h: H, c: Caller) -> Result[KeyScope, Capacity]` runs the place's policy inside `bound bytes n` + `bound steps s` + `isolate`. **Transact:** inside the head's `run`, before the body, on a view of the same `mut Tx` in the outermost scope. Then `store.narrow(mut tx, scope, body)`. So the policy reads the state the body writes over, in one serializable transaction. **Query:** inside the same `look`, at the same snapshot. Then `store.narrow_view(tx, scope, body)` (STORE-SPEC v2.26 AJ1). The policy's reads go to the same read log as the body's. **Fail closed:** if the policy faults, runs out of bytes or steps, or returns `Error`, the body never runs. A transact call is then answered `Failed(f)` by the runner's reply-only commit, which is recorded (§7) like any fault. A query's Subscribe or re-run gives `Ended(sub, Failed(f))`. **A denial is data, not an outcome:** a caller with no rights gets a scope with no entries (`store.none(tx)`). The body's first read or write then gets `OutOfScope`, and the body reports that through its own `E` or fault. No Outcome or Refusal kind is added. **Entry signatures change:** transact is `fn(mut Tx, H, Caller, take A) -> Result[R, E]` and query is `fn(Tx, H, Caller, take A) -> Result[Cover, Capacity]`. The body gets the Caller by view to write it into rows (an author column) or to narrow further. **The Key is unchanged**: `code.identity` already changes with the signature. | store.hirsel `narrow` :519, `look` :4268 |
| 5 | A Cover lies in its scope | **NEW. /wholehog: a Cover obeys `range`'s rule at the call that makes it** | `cover(tx: Tx, t: Shape[K, V], lo, hi) -> Result[Cover, store.Error]` takes the query's Tx, and `OutOfScope` is checked exactly as for `range` (STORE AE4 b). The whole encoded span must lie within the union of the scope's entries for `t`, of any access. **Why a check at all:** a Cover names rows **without reading them** (§4: they are not in the read set). So without this rule, a query could name the rows of a room it may not read. Its scope would never be consulted, and G2 would send every change inside that span. It replaces `store.key_span`, whose only caller is `cover`, with `store.span(tx, t, lo, hi)`, which does the same check. | runner.hirsel:470; store.hirsel:2482 |
| 6 | Revocation | **NEW. ATTACK (the FOR-SAM 73 reading)** | **Invariant S:** every row a session receives in a frame at version v lies in a Cover computed, with its policy, at some u ≤ v where no read of that run (the policy's included) changed in (u, v]. So the row lies in the caller's scope as evaluated at v. **Why it holds:** the policy's reads are in the subscription's read set (item 4). A commit b that changes any of them is a read-set hit in G2. The query and its policy re-run at b, giving a new scope and a new Cover ⊆ that scope (item 5). The diff of old Cover@a against new Cover@b sends a removal for every row that left, in the round's Batch at b. A sub whose read set was not hit has an unchanged scope, by item 3's premise. **Ordering, so that revoked coverage is retired before any value from b is sent:** (a) the client applies a Batch whole, removals included, before it shows any value from it (TS-SDK, stated now); (b) an `Ended(sub, ..)` produced in round b is queued by the owner before that round's Batch, and the client drops every row it holds for that sub when it applies an `Ended` (stated now). **Queued data (FOR-SAM 73, "authorization is re-checked before queued data is released"). Possibly Sam's call.** Frames at a may still wait in the owner's outgoing queue when round b's content arrives. Two readings: **(i) recommended:** "re-checked" means checked at the version the data carries. Frames at a hold rows that were in scope at a. They go out in order, and b's removals follow in the next frame.
